  • Lucifer: her patient. He cares more than he admits. His tail stayed stiff when he asked if she was with them.
  • Orion: a friend she brought water to. He’s guilty he never got to know her.
  • Her brother: her ghost, and the maker of the whistle.

Is she dead? The book says so through Orion, Kage, and the narration. But there’s no body (“there was just…nothing to find”). She “burst completely into light.” The narration says “Not all of Abby’s abilities are known” and that “Some think she may actually have fallen from heaven!” And the section named after her continues for nine chapters after she’s gone. I discuss this in Theories. My honest view: whether or not she returns, her death has to stay real in its consequences, or it will undercut the best chapter in the book.

What I’d push on. This draft gave her the image I asked for (the folded hands). Her brother still needs one detail of his own: a name, a laugh, what his enhancement was. He built a whistle that only his sister can hear. That’s a person with a personality, and the book can show it in one sentence.

Rating 9.5/10 She’s in fewer chapters than anyone in the main cast, and she’s the one the book is built around.

Ailani Shoma

At a glance. Latina, Black, and Japanese. Curly hair, often in a bun. Tanned skin. Pink workout clothes. A purple, fluorescent dorm room with string lights. Swears in Spanish when furious (“HIJO DE PUTA,” “PENDEJO,” “IMBÉCIL,” “CRETINO,” “CALLENSE”). Has friends in general studies, and is friends with Geneki. Dances alone in the mirror room. Wants to be a top choreographer and dancer, and joined the hero course to be “dependable” for her family and to fund that dream.

Power: Crystal Release. A chromatic sigil appears on her hands. The sigil turns ninety degrees for telekinesis. She makes, shapes, and controls crystals from nothing: projectiles, walls, weapons up to a war hammer, and delicate things like a butterfly whose wings flap. Range: 25 meters. Large constructs last 10 to 15 seconds. Small ones last almost indefinitely. Cost: overuse locks her hands in crystal from the fingertips to the wrists. S4 Ch7 shows the cost in full, and shows her turning it into armor: she blocks Lance’s saw-blade with her own half-crystal hand.

Arc so far. Ailani has spent the book reacting to other people’s drama, and the book knows it. She keeps getting cut off (“This isn’t about you right now”). She keeps holding her tongue because Kage or Orion asks her to. She’s “tired of being reactionary.” Until S4 Ch7 she had no real fight: her first-round opponent fell over on purpose. Her section opens with her past and builds to her walking out to perform. Then she wins the best-choreographed fight in the book by counting the tempo of Lance’s blade (“Ailani had been counting beats her whole life”) and by using a rule from her friend’s worst moment (“Gen’s my friend, after all, so I watched that replay way more times than she’d like”). She’s also, quietly, the book’s best detective. She’s watching Kage (“squint[ing] harder, looking for an imperfection”) and Orion (“weirdly relieved”) at the same time.

The romance. She dated Kage in first year. It started with a crystal to the gut and honey toast, and a napkin with a heart. She broke up with him because he did nothing and flirted with everyone. She took him back for one weekend, then dumped him again over Reina. She mourned him and hit him when he came back. “Keep my number.” She asks if he and Abby were ever together, which is jealousy. And she’s been spending every day with Orion, holding his hand and rooming with him, not noticing he blushes.

What makes her work. She’s funny, perceptive, and physically confident. She reads Orion’s control precisely (“That speed is like a light jog for Orion”). She calls out Inazuma’s racism when nobody else does. And she’s the only person who can calm Kage with a touch. The book also gives her a life outside heroism (dance, family, general-studies friends), which makes her the most grounded person in the cast.

Key lines.

  • “Dull tools only get duller without someone like me to sharpen them!”
  • “We aren’t back together yet.”
  • “And, Kage, lose my number.” / “And Kage? Keep my number.”
  • “I think he’s…trying to prove he’s better. Not the better fighter, better in every category.”
  • “EVERYONE CALLENSE!”
  • “We should show off so he picks one of us instead.”
  • “I’m just surprised no one else was paying attention to that.”
  • “This is my time, and I’ve been dying to perform.”

What I’d watch. Last time I said her section needed three things: a fight that shows what her power can do, a moment where her dancing matters, and a choice that’s about her and not about Kage. S4 Ch7 delivered the first two in one move (“Two counts to her one”). The third is still owed. Her match against Orion is the place for it: she’s fighting a friend who lied to her, and she’s spent a whole day suspecting him.

Prediction. Last time I predicted “She beats Lance with a fight that’s half dance. Then she faces Orion.” That’s exactly what happened, so I’ll extend it: she beats Orion, with her hands still damaged from Lance, and the fight forces the love triangle into the open. Then the grand final is Kage against Ailani, which pays off “I don’t want to fight you” (S2 Ch3) and “if we fought, I’d kick your ass.”

Rating 9.5/10 (up from 9). Out of the wings, and the best fight in the book is hers.

Character Dossiers: The Class

Lucifer Tulivalo (“Lu,” “Luci”)

At a glance. Tall (an inch shorter than Orion, which annoys him), orange hair, light blue eyes, dark red horns, and a tail that betrays his feelings: it wags when he’s happy, goes stiff when he’s worried. Pale skin. A dorm with one bedroom, medals, a trophy case, and a black claymore engraved “Minkowski.” He came first in the entrance exam. He’s “the top second-year hero student in the world,” which is literally true since there’s only one program. Tuli and valo are Finnish for “fire” and “light,” so his name is “light-bringer firelight.” That’s a lot of light for a boy carrying a demon.

Power: Hellspawn. He can produce fire from any part of his body (fists, horns, tail, eyes, mouth) at any temperature. “He can fine-tune the heat to a surgical burn or crank it up to an inferno that melts steel like paper!” And inside him is Abaddon.

The secret. He never fully sleeps, because sleep lets Abaddon out. That’s why he’s always tired and why Abby saw something draining him. He’s cruel on purpose: “I…crank my worst traits up to eleven so people don’t want to talk to me.” He keeps three people close: Elias, who knows and watches him; Reina, whose power can absorb his heat; and, from a distance, Abby, whom he pushed away to keep her safe.

Arc so far. He’s the school’s bully and the book’s most sympathetic villain-adjacent student. He threatens to burn Kage and Abby alive. He tells Ailani about Reina. He makes a deal with Anubis to end Kage’s rise. He breaks Reina’s gear and ignores her after winning. He burns Kage’s face assuming the nurses can fix it. Then, inside his own head, he finally accepts the demon as part of himself, mouths “I’m sorry” to Elias, and lets Abaddon out on live television. He loses anyway. Afterward he confesses the deal. And he votes to keep Kage in the school.

Key lines.

  • “Listen to me, you sticky fucking freak.”
  • “I know nothing is free. What’s your angle?”
  • “Angels can’t befriend demons.”
  • “She can take my heat. Abby would just get burned.”
  • “I don’t want her hanging around and ending up hurt!”
  • “Only Min gets to call me that.”
  • “I exposed myself in front of millions of people and you couldn’t just lose?!”

What makes him work. The book gives him a reason for every cruelty, and it doesn’t use the reason to excuse him. He hurts Reina. He hurts Abby. He hurts Kage. And he’s also a kid who hasn’t slept properly in years. The tail is a brilliant device: an honest body part on a dishonest person. This draft adds its best use yet. When Lucifer asks Anubis “What’s in it for me?”, “his tail started to sway. It moved slowly and heavily, like it belonged to something much bigger than him.” The tail isn’t only Lucifer’s tell anymore. It’s Abaddon’s.

What I’d watch. His motive for telling Ailani about Reina is the one cruelty without a stated reason. And now that the world has seen Abaddon, Lucifer’s future (his place in the program, his relationship with Anubis, how the public treats him) is the biggest open question in the student cast.

Prediction. Lucifer ends up with Huoyan, his fire-power counterpart, as Anubis’s “you’ll officially be Huoyan’s problem” line suggests. Huoyan is the one adult equipped to teach him to live with a fire that wants out. And I think Lucifer and Kage end up as reluctant allies, two monster-carriers who understand each other.

Rating 9/10

Abaddon

At a glance. Ten feet tall. Black skin, scorched bone, a tail “thick as a tree trunk,” blood-red eyes, taloned feet, horns tipped with fire. Speaks about himself in the third person, in capital letters, when he’s free. In Lucifer’s mind he sits in a child’s chair at the kitchen table of Lucifer’s childhood home, and speaks in the first person. His name is the Hebrew word for “destruction” and the angel of the abyss in Revelation.

Power. Everything Lucifer has, plus mass, strength, skin too thick for concrete fists, and enough heat to vaporize glue. Weaknesses: he needs air, and he can be knocked out.

What he wants. To be out. “I am tired of living my life watching yours through this domestic kitchen window!” He argues he and Lucifer “are one” and that Lucifer’s exhaustion comes from Abaddon trying to escape. He’s been out before, “after years,” which means some earlier escape.

Key lines.

  • “FREEDOM IS SWEETER THAN ABADDON COULD HAVE EVER IMAGINED!”
  • “You wear those devil horns, but you won’t accept your true self?”
  • “You’re a secondary player in Kage Ozul’s story.”
  • “Your selective intelligence confounds me. So smart, and so stupid.”
  • “The truth is so terrible that you need to make all of this look like your grand plan?! OH, I LIKE YOU!”

What makes him work. He’s funny, articulate, and right about people. He reads Kage’s self-deception in one line. He’s what Kage could become if the biomass wins.

Rating 9/10

Elias Minkowski (“Min”)

At a glance. Short (about an inch taller than Reina), jet-black hair, glasses (Rhatella-made, broken by Kage, not yet replaced), a boring outfit. Lucifer’s best friend and rival for the top of the class. “The quietest student in the sophomore class.” He seems to know when people are lying. He sleeps over in Lucifer’s room. He insists the claymore with his family name on it hangs perfectly straight. Minkowski is the name of the mathematician behind “Minkowski spacetime,” and Elias’s power stops things in spacetime. That’s a great name match.

Power: Chronal Inertia. Anything he touches stops “dead in spacetime.” Forces acting on it are banked and released all at once when he lets go. Each hold has a three-minute clock that burns only when the world pushes back; living things can struggle against it. Each extra hold is another live calculation in his head, which has given him “superhuman perception and razor-sharp processing.” Vital functions continue under a hold, which is how Kage beat him.

Arc so far. He’s cold, analytical, and dismissive of Kage, whom he humiliates on camera (“for food instead of the goodness in his heart”). He loses. He refuses Kage’s friendship in the most precise breakdown of Kage’s secret in the book, and in this draft he says he felt it: “What I felt under my hold was in your body. Something in you was pushing against my enhancement while you sat perfectly still.” “Either… illogical, or… hiding something. The worst part is, I believe the answer is both.” He apologizes to his friends for losing. When Abaddon appears, he runs to the bathroom shaking. He punches Lucifer for summoning the demon, deliberately unfrozen so Lucifer feels every hit. He hugs Kage. And he says: “You know I never wanted to feel that feeling again.”

The history. Lucifer: “Me and Elias have history.” Anubis’s file: “Not showing signs of trauma.” Abaddon: “Being out again after years.” The Hellspawn card: “Even fewer have lived after seeing it.” Elias: “I was worried that the demon might get out and I wouldn’t be there to stop it!” Something happened when they were children, Abaddon got out, and Elias was there.

Key lines.

  • “If I said it’s crooked, then it’s crooked, Luci.”
  • “You are strong. That’s very admirable.”
  • “Most people look at a car crash as they drive by, but what do they do after? They drive off.”
  • “I cannot be friends with someone I cannot trust.”
  • “We hate him and don’t trust him, but we’re not gonna kick him out with some stupid popularity contest.”

What makes him work. He looks like a cold genius and turns out to be a frightened, loyal kid who has made himself his best friend’s jailer. Every cold thing about him reads differently once you know that.

What I’d watch. He’s the one person who could figure out Kage’s secret on his own, and after S3 Ch12 he has physical evidence: something inside Kage pushed back against a time-stop. He’s more than halfway there. He’s also now the one classmate who has felt both monsters up close (Abaddon as a child, the biomass in a hold). Use that.

Prediction. The claymore belonged to someone in Elias’s family who was hurt or killed in the earlier Abaddon escape. Lucifer keeps it on his wall as penance or as a promise. And Elias’s power is the one thing that can hold Abaddon still.

Rating 9.5/10 The best-designed supporting character.

Reina Cruz (“Cruz”)

At a glance. Small, brown hair (it was white in the last draft), sparkling dark blue eyes, strong posture (“She stood tall for her size”), thin arms. Lucifer’s girlfriend. Elias’s friend. Says “BOOM!” with her whole body. Reina Cruz is Spanish for “queen” and “cross,” so the devil is dating the cross.

Power: Heat Charge. She absorbs heat and converts it into muscle mass. The more she absorbs, the bigger and stronger she gets, and the less mobile. Punching burns it off, and she shrinks. Empty, she’s dizzy and immobile. She uses a Rhatella-made pack on her back to store and release heat on command (shrink to dodge, regrow to grab). “The only one in the world that can truly say they can handle Lucifer’s heat.”

Arc so far. She’s the cheerful one in Lucifer’s group, and the one nobody tells the truth to. She wins her first match without understanding how. She absorbs her boyfriend’s flames and throws him around in the funniest fight in the tournament. He breaks her gear and ignores her after winning. She learns about Abaddon from the TV, along with the rest of the world. She sits with Kage’s group “as long as it takes till they apologize.” In S4 Ch7 she’s the first person in the lobby to understand how Ailani won (“Woah, that’s crazy smart!”), because she’s the one who won that way against Geneki. It’s a small moment, and it’s the first time since the bus that the book lets her be smart instead of left out.

Key lines.

  • “I’ll get you like BOOM!!!!”
  • “That…could’ve been me…”
  • “Whether we get in the Elite Program or not, we’ll still have each other!”
  • “Awww, babe you shouldn’t have!”
  • “So, both of you knew about this and kept it from me?!”

What makes her work. Her power is the perfect counter to her boyfriend’s, which explains why he keeps her close. And the book lets her be hurt in a specific way: not by the demon, but by being kept out.

What I’d watch. She’s at a turning point. She could break up with Lucifer, or demand to be let into the secret. Either way, she shouldn’t go back to being the cheerful one without consequence.

Rating 8.5/10

Rhatella Gardner (with C.O.R.E.)

At a glance. Four foot eight, brown skin, a cleft lip, metallic wires growing from her head, swirling goggles, rat-like ears. A cyan bodysuit and a lab coat. Hisses when startled. Reads romance novels. Ships everyone. Stamps every invention with a cartoon of her own face. Lives in a dorm that’s bigger on the inside, like “five power plants mashed into one.” Daughter of Gene Gardner. Sister of Geneki.

Power: Wireplay. “Rhatella’s body is a living forge of wires!” She grows and controls cables like limbs, slips them into machines to seize circuits, runs electricity harmlessly across her skin, unleashes “crackling bursts of energy,” and senses disruptions through any wired device. (The last draft’s card added that she “might one day manipulate solid metal.” That line is gone in this draft.)

C.O.R.E. (Centralized Operational Response Engine) runs the dorm. Its objectives are to process stimuli and respond, protect its creator, and protect itself and the facility. It took Kage’s blood. It sighs. It once kicked Kage down a shaft with a giant boot.

Her gear in the tournament. Elias’s glasses (broken by Kage), Rebento’s gumball machines, Lance’s hilt and a spare hilt (S4 Ch7), and Reina’s heat pack (broken by Lucifer). She builds alphabetically and is stuck on “O” for Ozul because of his “human insulator” body.

Arc so far. She’s lonely. “As long as I’m useful, right?” Orion becomes her first real friend. Kage becomes another. She watches her inventions get broken on live TV, cries, ships the boys again, and admits: “I wish I was the one they were all fighting over… Lord knows he wouldn’t take my heart!”

What makes her work. She’s the book’s comic relief with real sadness built in. Kage’s description of her to Lance is the best summary: “The only thing she loves doing is creating gadgets for people because that’s the only way she feels close to them.”

What I’d watch. She’s been offstage since Section 1 apart from the interlude. She has Kage’s blood from before the biomass. She’s the one person who could compare it to his blood now. That’s a loaded gun on her lab bench.

Prediction. Rhatella finishes Kage’s gear, and it’s built to handle something she doesn’t understand yet, the biomass. Or her analysis of his old blood sample reveals something about his power’s real nature.

Rating 8.5/10

Severine Neant

At a glance. Five foot eleven, bluish-violet hair, purple clothes and eyes. Speaks formally and in lists of “dislikes”: disruptions, incompetence, foolishness. Laughs until she cries. Drinks tea. Hasn’t spoken to a classmate in a year and a half, except Kage. Néant is French for “nothingness,” which fits someone whose power pushes everything away.

Power: Repulse-Redux. Repulsive force fields: barriers, redirection, flight, shockwaves, launched objects, focused blasts. Costs: stamina, weakening with prolonged use, concentration for complex fields, and recoil against powerful attacks.

Arc so far. She beats Kage cleanly, steps on his chest, and tells him to come back when he’s “significantly superior.” She loses in Round 1 to Lucifer’s push without a scratch on her, has a public meltdown, and screams for the only classmate she ever talked to. He turns away. She watches the rest at home and decides their rematch is “inevitable.”

What makes her work. She’s a perfectionist whose perfection isolates her completely, and the meltdown shows it. Her calling Kage’s name in front of everyone is the saddest moment in the first round.

What I’d watch. The meltdown is very long and very loud. The reader should never feel the book is laughing at her. Her return, presumably the rematch, should give her dignity.

Prediction. Severine gets into the Elite Program despite losing in Round 1, because Gene or Anubis bends the rule, and it causes resentment. Or she doesn’t, and the rematch with Kage happens outside the system.

Rating 8/10

Zulfiya Shifo

At a glance. A sophomore classmate with blue (or light blue) hair that she moves “like fingers.” Calls Huoyan “Niao Xiansheng,” surname first with the Chinese honorific for “teacher.” Calls Kage “King,” “your highness,” “your majesty,” “my sovereign,” and now “Prince.” Hates egos, except Kage’s. Zulfiya is a common Uzbek name, and shifo means “healing” or “cure” in Uzbek.

Power: Mending Mane. Healing hair that wraps people and restores wounds and stamina. It also reads bodies precisely: temperature to the degree, unconsciousness to the minute. The hair can be ripped, and burned or torn hair grows back slowly.

Arc so far. She heals Kage twice. She makes the King nickname stick. She forfeits her shot at the Elite Program to “support” him, which detonates his trauma. She’s the one who tells him he can’t regrow a lost part. She keeps noticing the whistle.

What makes her work. She’s a second healer in a book that just lost its first. Kage’s reaction to her “support” shows how badly Abby’s death broke him.

What I’d watch. Her forfeit cost her the Elite Program. That’s a real sacrifice, and it deserves to come back. Does she regret it? Does Kage feel responsible?

A new question. In this draft, she keeps her hair away from him on purpose. Before the fight she’s “tucking her blue hair behind her ear.” After it, Ailani complains that “No hair wrapped around him even a little.” On the walk back, “It stayed tucked behind her ear the whole walk, and not a single strand drifted his way.” And when he walked out to face her, “the smile seemed off.” Her hair diagnoses bodies. She healed him twice before the biomass. If she touched him now, she’d know something changed. I think she forfeited at least partly so she’d never have to touch him in front of the world. See Theories.

Rating 8/10

Cheyenne Bandalore (“Partner,” “Che”)

At a glance. Unbuttoned blazer, white blouse, a loosened red necktie, a crimson-and-gold serape, spurred boots, and a cowboy hat “she never took off.” Shadowed eyes with “the weary intensity of a desert hawk.” A strawberry lollipop held like a cigar. A metallic jingle, “either keys or pocket change.” Southern slang: “partner,” “bless your heart,” “two shakes of a lamb’s tail,” “I reckon.” Lance’s girlfriend.

Power: unknown. Her power card is literally cut off mid-sentence when Elias freezes her. She put her hands in her pockets to prepare her attack. She thinks her power probably can’t do telekinesis.

Arc so far. She’s the one who noticed Kage’s tears during the King speech and decided to believe him. She tells him to “take the time to enjoy being a prince.” She loses to Elias in seconds and hides how bad she feels about it. She lends Kage her hat, by force, and is the stake in his bet with Lance.

What makes her work. She’s a cowboy in a superhero school, and the book plays it straight enough that she becomes a person rather than a costume. The hat is a great prop.

Prediction. Her power has something to do with what’s in her pockets: coins, or something she throws or loads, like a gunslinger. The jingle is the clue. Please don’t explain it until it’s dramatic.

Rating 8/10

Lance Faisceau (“Fai,” “Lancey,” “Sir Lance”)

At a glance. Gray hair that reflects light. Stern face. Says he’s class representative; Rebento says Lance is vice-rep. Dates Cheyenne. Has money (“rich boy”). Calls everyone by their last name until he likes them. Faisceau is French for “beam,” which fits his beam swords, and the “Lance” and “Avalon” pairing makes him a Lancelot figure.

Power: Avalon’s Legacy. Beam swords of yellow energy in any shape. Across the book: rapier, greatsword, arming sword, cruciform sword, stiletto, gladius, claymore, wakizashi, cutlass, cinquedea, odachi, falchion, and a basket-hilted broadsword. The blade “saws through almost anything rigid,” and it saws rather than slices: “his blade was more like a cast saw.” Flesh “moves with the blade instead of parting for it,” so a hit lands as a searing strike. His Rhatella-made hilt protects his hands, and he carries a spare. He can form blades without holding the hilt, which nobody knew until S4 Ch7. “There seems to be a limit to Lance, a point where he decides that he needs to stop even when his hilt and hands are fine, but people aren’t quite sure why!”

King Arthur. The limit is a state he calls King Arthur. His eyes glow yellow, then turn completely yellow. “He became so fight-starved that he was literally blinded by his desire for combat. He lost his sight, but his other senses sharpened.” His fighting gets “significantly less refined,” and he gets “more anxious to strike first instead of planning.” When the fight ends, he snaps back and laughs it off: “Sorry if I got a little carried away there at the end.” This is what he meant in S4 Ch3 by “when fighting sometimes you end up as someone else.”

Backstory. His father was a high-earning villain in Quebec who taught him to steal, run heists, and break in, and who spent time with him only then. The father was arrested. Lance defended him, until he watched a frog approach some ants on their way home and turned the frog toward the pond without thinking. He realized he’d hated every heist. “I try to hold people to the standard I didn’t hold to myself.”

Arc so far. He helps Kage beat Lucifer to the door: “Heroes help other heroes.” He objects to “win by any means.” He knights Volkan. He refuses Kage’s apology until he sees honorable fighting, then becomes Kage’s closest new friend after one night of talking. He casts the first vote to keep Kage.

Key lines.

  • “Heroes help other heroes.”
  • “Fight them how you’d want to be fought.”
  • “Oh, the straight man doesn’t get to be funny too? You’re putting me in slots, Kage.”
  • “Okay, Anubis.”
  • “I trust Kage deeply.”
  • “When fighting sometimes you end up as someone else.”
  • “Sorry if I got a little carried away there at the end.”

What makes him work. He’s the moral center of Section 3 and the clearest mirror for Kage: a villain’s son choosing heroism, beside a hero choosing villains in secret. After S4 Ch7 the mirror has a second surface. Lance carries something that takes over in a fight, just like Kage and Lucifer. The difference is that Lance has spent years learning to stop before it does. The honor code isn’t only a reaction to his father. It’s a leash. The knight who lectures everyone about honor is the one who knows best what he becomes without it. And the thing he becomes is named after a king, in a book where Kage wants to be King Kage and Cheyenne joked, “If Lance can knight people, wouldn’t that make him a king?”

What I’d watch. Last time I wrote that his “limit” and “you end up as someone else” were connected, and that the Ailani match would show it. Both held up. My guess about what it was didn’t (see Prediction). The next question is why he let King Arthur take him against Ailani when he’d held it back all tournament, including against Volkan. My read: the fight was so even, and so much fun, that he stopped deciding to stop. That’s the scariest thing about him, and the most human.

Prediction. Last time I predicted Lance’s limit was “a fear of becoming his father.” That was wrong. It’s a literal berserker state, not a moral line. I’d keep half of it, though: a boy whose father taught him to fight dirty, and who has a second self that fights without thinking, has probably been told those two things are related. I still think his father will turn out to be connected to the villain organization, because a “high earning villain” and “the boss” live in the same world. And I think Lance will be the one who teaches Kage how to stop before the biomass takes over, because he’s the only person in the book who has practiced it.

Rating 9.5/10 One night in a hotel room made him one of the best characters in the book.

Rebento Azucre (“Sugar Bomb”)

At a glance. Brown hair. Says he’s the main class representative. Pops gumballs. Single, and Lance tells him “your relationships don’t last longer than a week.” He introduced Cheyenne and Lance. Crude (“Were her lips cold during kissing too?”). Protective of his friends. Azucre is Galician for “sugar,” and rebentar is Portuguese for “to burst,” so his name is close to “sugar burst.”

Power: Sweet-Toothed Surprise. He turns the glucose in non-living things into kinetic charge, so candy becomes bombs. Gumball machines on his belt (Rhatella-made), a sprinkle shaker, jawbreakers, hard candies, and a hidden cherry gumball in a knuckle-cracking fist. Telekinesis, signaled by rainbow swirl eyes. He can’t charge living things, and charging costs him energy. Explosions smell like burning caramel.

Arc so far. He helps throw Kage over the crowd. He blows Alex out of the ring. He defends Cheyenne from Orion. He’s the only one who says out loud that Kage’s sudden healing makes no sense and calls a vote to remove him, which fails unanimously. He loses to Orion, accuses Kage of coaching, and moves to sit with Lucifer and Elias.

What makes him work. He’s right about Kage, and nobody believes him. That makes him the book’s Cassandra, and that’s a good role to give a crude, funny character.

What I’d watch. As noted, the book is at risk of making Rebento unlikeable for being correct. Give him a scene where his suspicion comes from fear for his friends, not ego.

Prediction. Rebento finds real evidence of Kage’s villain connection, maybe through a sighting or a detail from the night of the kidnapping, and has to decide whether to use it.

Rating 8/10

Inazuma Sokudo

At a glance. Blue hair, dark blue eyes (“the red to my blue”). Loud: “WAZZZUPPPPPPPP!!!” Knew Kage in middle school in Japan. Texted Kage all through first year, including the maid-outfit photo. Calls Orion “Big Vance” and a “big black dolt.” Inazuma is Japanese for “lightning,” and sokudo means “speed.”

Power: Lightning Fast. Super-speed in blue light. Friction stores static electricity in his body, which he releases as lightning blasts. If he doesn’t discharge, he burns out. A misstep can send him flying the wrong way.

Arc so far. He’s the “another speedster” from Kage’s S1 Ch2 complaint, and the maid-photo sender from S1 Ch5. He makes racist comments to Orion, refuses to apologize, uses them as a weapon in the match, and loses to a low blow. He’s out of the Elite Program.

What makes him work. He’s a real kind of person, the friend from home who knew you before and won’t let you change. Ailani’s “He kind of reminds me of you” is the most uncomfortable true thing anyone says to Kage.

What I’d watch. He made racist comments and faced only a low blow. Orion apologized; Inazuma didn’t. If the book wants that injustice to stand, fine. But someone, ideally Kage, should eventually say clearly that Inazuma was wrong, because Kage’s defense of him is one of Kage’s real failures.

Rating 7.5/10 He’s effective, and unpleasant on purpose.

Volkan Salyangozu (“Knight Volkan”)

At a glance. Big, with dark red eyes, always in a black hoodie. Shy. Late to class in Section 1, punished with fifty laps, and forgotten by Kage for two sections. Volkan is Turkish for “volcano,” and salyangoz is Turkish for “snail.”

Power: Volcano Snail. He’s built like a scaly-foot gastropod, a real deep-sea snail with an iron-plated shell. He has a massive iron shell on his back, tucks and rolls, tolerates extreme heat, and has gills. “He may be slow to move, but he tries to think fast!”

Arc so far. All he wants is “to last long enough for them to remember his name.” He loses to Lance by choice, after Lance stops him from rolling out, offers him a free win, and he refuses (“I want to earn it! I’m not just the slow kid!”). He’s knighted in front of the crowd, and “Now the whole stadium couldn’t stop shouting it.” He comes to wish Kage luck, which moves Kage more than anything else.

What makes him work. He’s a running joke turned into one of the tournament’s most moving moments.

Prediction. His heat resistance makes him a natural match for Lucifer or Abaddon. Watch for him to matter in a fire-related crisis.

Rating 8/10

Geneki Gardner (“Gen”)

At a glance. About eight feet tall, extremely muscular, a lazy eye, rat ears like Rhatella’s, and a tail that’s a cut-off stump. “Doesn’t show emotion much.” Ailani’s friend: “She’s a very nice girl.” Gene’s daughter.

Power. Strength enhanced by green energy. A stomp that “nearly disintegrated” the concrete floor.

Arc so far. She loses Round 1 by breaking the floor so hard she’s technically underneath it. Her mother corrects her on live TV. She asks to go home, “Alone, preferably.” Then her loss wins Ailani’s semi-final. “Gen’s my friend, after all, so I watched that replay way more times than she’d like.” Gene announces it as “my wonderfully splendid daughter Geneki’s failure.” Her mother still can’t say it kindly.

What makes her work. She’s barely on the page and still one of the saddest characters. The stump tail, the public correction, and “alone, preferably” all point to a story about her mother.

Prediction. The tail is a story, possibly an injury her mother’s ambitions caused, or a surgery. Geneki is one of the strongest people in the class and one of the least interested in being seen. She could be important later.

Rating 7.5/10 Small role, big potential.

Youseff Nasser

At a glance. About 5’3”, brown-skinned. Twiddles his thumbs and picks his nose in front of a crowd chanting his family name. Anubis’s younger brother. Doesn’t want to be a hero.

Power: unknown. His power is never shown.

Arc so far. At the bell, he shouts “OH NO! I’ve tripped and fallen out of bounds!” and flops onto the grass. He tells Ailani the Elite Program “sounds like torture,” lets her crystal hit him, and laughs at his brother. Huoyan smirks. Elias leaves him off the list of classmates he studied.

What makes him work. He’s an act of sabotage with legs, and the funniest rebuke Anubis gets in the book.

Prediction. Elias skipping him is a clue. Either Youseff’s power is unreadable, or Elias doesn’t think he matters, and he’s wrong. Anubis said “his heart’s just not in it,” which doesn’t mean his power isn’t strong. I’d watch Youseff.

Rating 7.5/10

Alexander Lund

At a glance. Green hair, flower earrings. Anxious. His mother cheers for him from the front row. Lund means “grove” in Scandinavian languages, which with the flowers and green hair suggests a plant power.

Power: unknown. He loses before anything is shown.

Arc so far. He loses instantly when he turns toward his mother. He stays at the tournament because his mother wants him to make friends, and rooms silently with Elias. In the Abaddon fight, he pushes his mother’s hand away so he can keep watching. When Kage sticks his tongue out at him, he laughs and starts the chant that turns the crowd.

What makes him work. He’s the ordinary kid whose laugh proves Kage’s heroism works.

Rating 8/10 for one perfect moment.

Character Dossiers: The Adults

Huoyan Niao (“Hellfeather,” “Mr. Niao,” “Niao Xiansheng,” “Hon”)

At a glance. Tall, pale, bright white hair, usually-hidden wings. A strict homeroom teacher who “loathe[s] all of my students equally.” English is his second language (Anubis corrects his grammar). He “barely drink[s] anymore.” He sleeps at the school (“I sleep here”). He runs his own agency. He used to be Anubis’s sidekick, and very likely more than that. Huǒyàn niǎo is Mandarin for “flame bird,” which is to say, phoenix.

Power: Phoenix. A “walking flame made flesh.” He radiates and fires heat. Flaming wing feathers can be shot as projectiles or used as a shield. He has a body alteration (the wings) and a controllable enhancement: “two in one.” Weakness: water on his wings puts the flames out for a while.

Arc so far. Every time the book shows Huoyan’s surface, it shows what’s underneath a page later. He wants to break up the first spar, then lets it go. He’s the teacher who forces fifty laps, and the one who panics when Kage collapses. He threatens and slams heads, and he admits he dreamed Kage and Orion “could go so far together.” He’s the only adult searching for Abby, refreshing his phone in the middle of the tournament, until Kelly takes it from him and, “For once, Huoyan didn’t reach for it.” He’s the only adult who tries to stop the Abaddon fight. He’s right about Kage’s math and wrong about Kage’s chances. And he’s the one who says “I haven’t told Anubis everything.”

Key lines.

  • “I have morons for students.”
  • “Teachers do not have favorites. I loathe all of my students equally. And I haven’t told Anubis everything.”
  • “I may not agree with Lucifer, but I defend my students.”
  • “My students are good children. They may be dysfunctional, but all of them are truly heroic.”
  • “One times one is less than zero point five times three.”
  • “I teach all heroes, not just ones who punch well… Pacifists make great rescue heroes.”
  • “I sleep here.”
  • “I am mildly contented by your return, Ozul.”

What makes him work. He’s the adult the book trusts. Gruff people who secretly care are common, but Huoyan is specific: an ex-sidekick who stayed when his partner became a brand, living at the school, teaching kids who mostly can’t stand him.

What I’d watch. “I haven’t told Anubis everything” is the book’s most explicit promise from an adult, and it’s unpaid. Huoyan knows something about Kage that Anubis doesn’t.

Prediction. Huoyan knows something about Kage’s power, or his history, that explains why a failing first-year student was kept in the top class. He’ll reveal it at the moment it can hurt or help the most. And when Lucifer is cut off by Anubis, Huoyan is the one who takes him in.

Rating 9.5/10 The best adult in the book.

Khaled Nasser (“Anubis,” “Number One,” “Mr. Know-it-all”)

At a glance. Tall, gray-haired, always in sunglasses. The number-one hero in the world. Head of the hero program. A bare, unpainted office with one decoration: an Anubis action figure. Says “Sigh” out loud. Calls Huoyan “Hon” while insisting they’re “simply coworkers.” Has a younger brother, Youseff. Named after the Egyptian god who guides the dead and weighs their hearts against a feather. Note that his old partner is named Hellfeather.

Power: unknown. In this draft the S1 Ch2 hint is a gesture: “Anubis aimed a finger gun at Kage’s back. Something sparked at the tip of his finger.” Elsewhere his presence has weight. “The air in the room grew heavier with every word” (S2 Ch9). His stare is “so gravitational that Kage felt he might fall through the floor down to the earth’s core” (S2 Ch15). The chapter that introduces him properly is “Anubis-pheric Pressure.” And the god he’s named for weighs hearts. See Theories.

What he does. He’s the program’s salesman. He wants cameras in the dorms (“despite my asking”). He keeps photo files on students without their knowledge, with notes on their trauma and potential. He asked to put the tournament on TV. He celebrates merch sales and record viewership, and says “That’s not important right now!” about a missing student. He accepts a deal to drop Kage if Lucifer gives him a good fight. He watches Kage break his own bones and holds Huoyan back: “Isn’t that what being a hero is all about?” He saw Kage on camera the night of the kidnapping and told no one. He knew about Abaddon. And he wants Kage as his successor “someday soon,” because “an orphaned failure turned number one hero just sells itself.”

And yet. He takes his sunglasses off only when no one is looking: after aiming the finger gun at Kage’s back in S1 Ch2, and after Huoyan storms out in S2 Ch9, when he “let out a breath that had been held way too long.” He’s also the first adult to call Kage “brilliant.” He talked Huoyan out of punishing him twice. He’s delighted, genuinely, when Kage challenges his authority. “You don’t put on a heroic act; you just act heroic” is the kindest thing anyone says about Kage.

Key lines.

  • “You two were brilliant.”
  • “The system is built this way for a reason.”
  • “Maybe both of them just needed mental health days.”
  • “Are you challenging my authority as head of the hero program?… That’s wonderful!”
  • “An orphaned failure turned number one hero just sells itself!”
  • “If I were the one who needed rescuing, I wouldn’t care if you needed to shoot the villain.”
  • “THAT IS HOW YOU PUT ON A SHOW!”

What makes him work. He’s the book’s thesis in a person. He isn’t evil. He’s a man who has confused being seen with being good, and he has the power to teach that to children.

What I’d watch. He’s very close to being a straight antagonist. He’ll be stronger if he keeps the warmth too, and if the reader can never be sure whether he’s playing a long game. This draft helped with the private breath in S2 Ch9. The one place he still reads flat is the Lucifer deal (“He was a fan of this negotiation”). “I was worried you might’ve been compromised” suggests he suspects Kage’s villain ties. Is he letting it happen? Using it?

Prediction. Anubis knows more about Kage’s involvement with villains than he admits, and is cultivating it, possibly to use Kage as a lead to the boss. The “someday soon” also suggests he has a reason to step down: an illness, an injury, or a plan. And the Security Accords, which would put cameras everywhere, have his fingerprints on them.

Rating 9/10

Gene Gardner

At a glance. Tall and rat-like, with large ears, a snout, and whiskers. A green suit, green glasses, and black hair. Creator of the Elite Tournament and the Elite Program. Loves announcing. Mother of Rhatella and Geneki.

Arc so far. She’s the tournament’s chaos agent. She makes the rules (“as stated in the rules, the ones I just made up”), changes the match order to undermine Anubis’s deal, skips the countdown entirely in S4 Ch7 (“Screw the count! Just go!”), and asks the crowd whether to continue. In this draft she also has a tell: when she hears Anubis trade Kage’s future to Lucifer, “Her smile stayed bright, but her whiskers gave one sharp twitch, and she didn’t look at Anubis once.” Two chapters later, “she still hadn’t looked at Anubis once.” And when Kage beats Elias: “Her whiskers gave one sharp twitch. So much for an easy veto.” She also corrects her daughter in public (“I thought I taught you better”) and tells Zulfiya to “go home if you want! I don’t care, as long as you move fast!”

Key lines.

  • “Did you forget this is MY tournament?”
  • “I’m not going to gamble away a kid’s future to try and save a possibly burning ship.”
  • “Anubis is the one who bet the kid’s future, not me. I just made him roll the dice before he gets paid.”
  • “Sticking it to Anubis is fun.”
  • “It’s not what I think. It’s what they think.”

What makes her work. She’s both the person who saves Kage from the deal and a parent whose daughter wants to be “alone, preferably.” The book lets her be both.

What I’d watch. Last time I said her reason for switching the order didn’t quite hold, and that she didn’t answer Huoyan. This draft gives her a non-answer that works: Anubis made the bet, she only made him “roll the dice before he gets paid.” And it reveals her real motive under the principle. If Elias wins fast, “It’d be an easy veto.” She was hoping Kage would lose so she wouldn’t have to decide about him. She’s less a principled referee than a woman with a veto and a grudge, and that’s more interesting.

Prediction. Last time I guessed Gene decides who gets into the Elite Program. The “veto” line confirms she has at least a veto. Her choices will be political, and she has now watched Kage regenerate on camera. I’d also still watch Geneki’s tail, and “For once, Gene didn’t sound like she was talking to the cameras.” There’s a mother under the announcer.

Rating 8.5/10 (up from 8).

Kelly Iasacht

At a glance. Blond, blue-eyed. A consistent top hero who wants Anubis’s spot. He gets less government money because he chose not to teach at the school. He has a wife and a son. The son “didn’t end up showing up.” He carries an “Irish remedy” in a vial. Iasacht is an Irish word (I believe it means something like “loan” or “foreign”).

Arc so far. He’s sent to escort Severine because he isn’t staff, so the program isn’t “fully liable.” He “had to use my enhancement on her,” which we still haven’t seen. He mocks Anubis. He chugs the beer Anubis offered Huoyan. He takes Huoyan’s phone and tells him to rest. He wakes the boys with smelling salts. He calls the remaining contestants pawns: “now every kid left is a pawn putting on a show for you, huh, Anubis?” And he reads rooms: passing a grinning Lucifer in the hall, he “glanced from Anubis’s smirk to Huoyan’s frown and decided he’d ask later.”

What makes him work. He’s the outsider pro who can say what Huoyan can’t, and his small kindness to Huoyan is a nice surprise.

Prediction. His son is a classmate, probably the twentieth student we haven’t met, and the reason he “didn’t show” will matter. This draft adds a clue. Huoyan tells him, “Good job not forcing your son to compete” (the last draft said “kid”). Huoyan teaches the class. If he knows the son and knows Kelly didn’t force him, the son is probably in Huoyan’s room.

Rating 7.5/10

Character Dossiers: The Villains

Thanatos Augustus (“Shifting Horror,” “Horror,” “Thany”)

At a glance. Six feet tall. Orange eyes. Black hair fading to white at the ends. A black suit and shirt, with a contrasting white tie that he fixes while he talks. (The last draft gave him purple eyes, a face-to-neck scar, and a habit of rubbing it, and called him Thanatos Shioya. All of that is gone. His surname is now Augustus, the title of Rome’s first emperor, which makes him the most imperial name in a book full of kings, princes, and knights.) “One of the most notorious villains currently on the underworld scene.” Thanatos is the Greek personification of death, which makes him a counterpart to Anubis.

Power: Bio-Forge. He controls his own cells: density, shape, and new appendages. Anything he touches, organic or not, can be absorbed. He regenerates, and physical attacks barely hurt him. Costs: too many transformations in a row make his body fail. Some materials “reject integration.” Losing focus brings migraines, then blackouts. Absorbed things leave “instincts” that scratch at his mind. And giving away biomass makes him smaller, weaker, and “starving.”

What he does. He finds Kage at his lowest, tells him the truth about his self-destruction, and recruits him with “a few favors.” He calls Kage a partner in public and a pawn in private. He takes an ice spike for Kage, then says he won’t save him again. He saves him again. He watches Kage’s limbs get broken and referees the game. He rebuilds Kage from his own body without consent, and leaves for Russia. When he sees Kage on TV, he wants to kill Frostbite.

Key lines.

  • “Animals that never stop running don’t evolve.” … “They die.”
  • “Do you want to run past me or run with me?”
  • “I am not one of your teachers, or your savior. And I’m certainly no hero.”
  • “He is a pawn to us, but he can’t know that.”
  • “Most predators tear their meal to shreds the moment they catch it… You? You deliver it untouched.”
  • “This mass inside you will amplify what’s already there.”

What makes him work. He’s right. Everything he tells Kage about Kage is true, and that’s why it works. And the rebuild shows him paying a real physical cost for his “project,” which keeps him from being simple.

What I’d watch. He’s been offstage since S2 Ch10, apart from Chapter NULL. When he comes back, he’s angry at Frostbite and has a “serious talk” pending with Nerezza. His return should change Kage’s situation immediately.

Prediction. Thanatos knows more about Kage than he lets on (“A boy who’s gone through what he has”). Either he was involved with the convenience store, or he’s been watching Kage for the boss for longer than one night in an alley.

Rating 9.5/10

Frostbite

At a glance. A glowing blue-and-black suit with a mask and compartments (clothes, makeup, contacts). Under it, pale skin with dark, blistered fingertips, “like the cold was eating him from the inside out.” He calls Thanatos “Horror” and “Thany.” Kage bows to him.

Power: Polar Shift. Total creation and control of ice. Cost: frostbite that spreads from the inside out. Weak to fire and explosions, “but it’s hard to beat someone who can build anything he can imagine.”

What he does. He calls Kage a pawn and nearly kills him. Then he apologizes, pats his head, brings him clothes and makeup, calls Thanatos weak, gives him a “test” (kidnap Lucifer), lingers over the unconscious Lucifer, lets Kage enter the tournament, bets on him, and says “He promised me he’d win. I’m holding him to it.” He also says “I’ll kill you if you do” about every forty pages.

Mysteries.

  1. Kage’s hand “curled around nothing” every time Frostbite is near, and once when Anubis said his name.

  2. “The other half, Frostbite would reveal to Kage later.”

  3. “I don’t care what he…” followed by “I don’t care what the guy in the chair does to me.”

  4. He uses Lucifer’s exact insult: “sticky fucking freak.”

  5. He “crouched beside [Lucifer] longer than required. He reached towards Lucifer’s face with his bare hand, then pulled it away.”

  6. The disguise contacts he hands Kage are yellow, Abby’s eye color. Coincidence, or does he know whose eyes those were?

What makes him work. He’s the book’s most contradictory adult. A murderer who brings a grieving kid eyeliner. His care is clumsy, threatening, and real. His pat on the head “was the first gentle touch Kage had felt since Abby,” and Kage starts preferring him to Thanatos for a simple reason: “At least with Frostbite, he always knew where he stood.”

Prediction. See Theories. I think Frostbite caused the convenience store attack, and his kindness to Kage is guilt. I also think he has a personal connection to Lucifer.

Rating 9.5/10 The character I most want to understand.

Nerezza

At a glance. A woman in black with a mask. Vain (“How dare you ruin my beautiful face”). Lives in a crappy apartment in Russia. Drinks coffee. Watches the news, and children screaming. Flirts with Thanatos. Nerezza is Italian for “blackness.”

Power: Umbrakinesis. She controls shadows as parts of her body, sees in the dark, moves silently, and travels through shadows. Her tentacles can be sharp, can have toothed suckers, and can come out of other people’s shadows. Limits: she can only go twenty miles from places she has walked. Overuse blinds her eyes, locks her hands, and floods her mind with pain. Too many jumps and the shadows “deny entry.” Light weakens her.

What she does. She assassinates an official who was pushing the Security Accords and destroys a camera. She tortures Kage and Orion, kills Abby, makes Orion choose, breaks all of Kage’s limbs in a game, and hands him to Thanatos “in near perfect condition.” Then she rewatches his screams on TV.

Key lines.

  • “I see why Thanatos likes you.”
  • “‘Sticky Situation! No longer bringing smiles!’”
  • “Run, boy.”
  • “Whoops! My finger slipped!”
  • “Children screaming in pain is next level television.”

What makes her work. She’s pure menace with specific limits. She’s the villain the book uses to make its strongest point: she watches children suffer for entertainment, and so, in a cleaner way, does the stadium.

What I’d watch. She “chose” Kage to pay for Abby’s explosion, and she was stopped from killing him (“The only reason you’re still alive is because-!”). Her history with Kage isn’t finished, and Orion’s secret is hers to expose.

Prediction. She’s the one who reveals Orion’s choice, and she’ll do it in a way designed to hurt both of them.

Rating 9/10 Genuinely frightening.

“The Guy in the Chair” (the boss)

At a glance. Unseen. Frostbite says “I don’t care what the guy in the chair does to me,” which is the kind of thing you say about someone you’re afraid of. He sends Thanatos to Russia (“The boss is sending me”) and Nerezza after politicians (“the boss has another job for you”). His organization opposes the Security Accords. Someone is keeping Kage alive (“I’m not sure if I can kill you yet”).

Prediction. “In the chair” could mean a wheelchair, a throne, or a prison. I’d bet he has a personal interest in Kage, which is why Thanatos “needs” him, why Nerezza can’t kill him, and why Frostbite was going to say “I don’t care what he…” before catching himself.

Minor and Unseen Characters

C.O.R.E.

Rhatella’s AI. It’s protective, monotone, and occasionally petty. It sighs at Kage and kicks him with a giant boot. It took Kage’s blood. It tells Orion, “if at any point I detect ill intent, I will not hesitate to terminate you.” It knows about Orion’s verbal warning before the school has filed it. It’s a great comic device that could become a plot device. Rating: 7.5/10.

The soul up above

The person crushed under the awning at the convenience store. Never named. Kage was holding their wrist. Kage’s entire heroism is a debt to them: “I need that soul up above looking down and knowing… that I became a real hero.” Cheyenne mixes them up with Abby when she sees the whistle, and in a sense, now there are two souls up above. Prediction: giving this person a name, a face, or a family later would be devastating, in a good way.

Abby’s brother

He was “incredibly sick.” He made a gold whistle only Abby can hear. He died because Abby was told to wait, and she “sat by his bed every night with her hands folded to pray, when she could have laid them on him instead.” He’s the reason Abby never waits. Wish: give him one specific detail of his own.

Lance’s father

A high-earning villain in Quebec who taught his son to steal. Arrested. The only time he spent with Lance was during crimes. Wish: let him be alive and reachable.

Alexander’s mother

“You can do it, sweetie!!! I’m so proud of you for trying!” She forces her son to stay and make friends. She covers his eyes, then joins his chant: “YEAH, KAGE! BEAT THAT DEVIL!” She’s the only unconditionally supportive parent in the book, and Kage stares at her. Rating: 9/10 for two lines.

Kelly’s wife and son

The wife yells at Kelly to hurry. The son “didn’t end up showing up,” and Huoyan praises Kelly for “not forcing your son to compete.” They’re seeds.

The murdered official

A man in a suit outside a government building, crushed under Nerezza’s foot. He was pushing the Security Accords. His replacement is next.

The drunk bettor

He lost $300 on “demon boy” to a man whose ice wouldn’t melt. “He your son or some shit?” It’s a funny line, and given everything in this book about fathers, a pointed one.

The crowd

The crowd is a character. It boos, cheers, chants “Nasser,” “Lucifer,” “Knight Volkan,” and “Kage,” and yells “KEEP GOING! THIS IS AWESOME!” while two boys bleed. It’s the thing Kage wants “forever.” It’s also the thing Alex turns, with one laugh, from bloodthirsty to supportive. The book is smart to treat it as fickle, powerful, and easily led.

Themes

1. Being seen versus saving people

This is the book’s central argument, covered in the Verdict. Every major adult takes a side. Anubis (watched): cameras, merch, TV, “THAT IS HOW YOU PUT ON A SHOW!” Huoyan (saving): “I teach all heroes, not just ones who punch well… Pacifists make great rescue heroes.” Nerezza takes the watching side to its extreme: “Children screaming in pain is next level television.” Kage holds both positions at once, and the biomass is tipping him toward the wrong one. S4 Ch7 adds a twist: for the first time, people perform for Kage. Ailani and Lance agree to “show off so he picks one of us instead.” The boy who wanted to be watched forever is now the audience.

2. Holding on and letting go

The book’s founding image is a hand closing on a wrist and then on air. Look at how often hands carry the story:

  • “His fingers were holding air.” (Prologue)
  • “All the alternate universes where his grip held.” (S1 Ch1)
  • “This time, he made sure his grip held.” (S1 Ch2)
  • He hugs Ailani “like he could hold on to the version of them that was already gone,” and when she pushes him off, “Kage could feel the emptiness in his hands again.” (S1 Ch3)
  • “A wrist in his grip, and then nothing but air.” (S1 Ch8)
  • His hand twitches when he meets Thanatos, and then he shakes that hand. “Thanatos’s grip closed around it firmly and held a beat too long.” (S1 Ch12)
  • The whistle handoff: “his hand stopped halfway… Her fingers stayed on it for a moment, and then let go for Kage’s fist to close around it.” (S2 Ch2)
  • “His hand curled around nothing” near Frostbite. (S1 Ch13, S2 Ch10, S2 Ch15)
  • Abby’s hands heal his knuckles. (S1 Ch1)
  • He clutches the whistle in nearly every scene of Sections 3 and 4.
  • His hand presses against the glass for Severine, then pulls away. (S3 Ch2)
  • His hands move toward the glass when he sees Alex’s mother. (S3 Ch7)
  • He breaks his own hands over and over against Abaddon. (S3 Ch16)
  • Ailani presses her hand to the glass for him. (S3 Ch15)
  • “You let go of my hand to ogle at a cake?… I won’t let go a second time!” (S4 Ch1)
  • Orion raises the whistle in a closed fist. (S3 Ch16)
  • “His hand balled into a fist at his side before he made it let go.” Orion, after “let them slide off you.” (S3 Ch5)
  • In the photo on Ailani’s dresser, “Kage’s arm was a smear, but whole. Orion’s thumb tightened on the frame.” (S2 Ch8)
  • Kage wakes from the rebuild reaching for “Abby’s backpack with one hand and the whistle in his pocket with the other.” (S2 Ch7)
  • Ailani blocks a saw-blade with her own half-crystallized hand. (S4 Ch7)

The phrase “his hand” appears 104 times in this draft (99 in the last one), and “hand” or “hands” 254 times. I don’t think that’s an accident. Hands are where this book keeps its feelings.

3. Adhesion and attachment

Kage’s power is to make things stick, and he’s someone who can’t stay attached. He “stopped unpacking his bag.” He dated nine women in a few months. He tells Orion they’re “not friends” and that Inazuma’s comments should “slide off,” and one chapter later he “tried to let [Lance’s parent comment] slide off him. The same thing he’d told Orion to do before.” It doesn’t. The boy who makes things stick can’t make anything slide. The book plays with this constantly, from the narration’s “Adhering to every rule, more or less. Lately, less” to Cheyenne’s “oil and water.” His arc is learning to stick to people, and the tragedy is that he’s learning it from villains first.

4. Atonement

Almost everyone is paying off a debt:

  • Kage: the store, then Abby. “It’s the only way to make up for the things I’ve done.”
  • Abby: her brother. “She sat by his bed every night with her hands folded to pray, when she could have laid them on him instead.”
  • Orion: the dome, twice. “He had made his choice at three. Now he was making it again.”
  • Lance: his father, and now King Arthur. “I try to hold people to the standard I didn’t hold to myself.”
  • Lucifer: Abaddon, and whatever happened to Elias.
  • Elias: possibly failing to stop the demon the first time.
  • Frostbite: “the other half,” which I suspect is the store.
  • Huoyan: whatever he and Anubis were, and whatever he “hasn’t told.”

The book is careful not to suggest any of these debts can be paid by winning a tournament, even though most of these characters are trying exactly that.

5. Consent and bodies

The book returns again and again to bodies being used without permission. C.O.R.E. takes blood. Anubis photographs students and wants cameras in their rooms. Thanatos puts himself into Kage, and Frostbite says it out loud: “You just invaded the boy’s body without consent and forced your biomass onto him.” (The last draft said “forced yourself onto him,” which made the assault metaphor explicit. The new line is more literal and keeps the point.) Abaddon lives in Lucifer’s body. Nerezza turns Abby’s own shadow into the weapon that kills her. The Security Accords would put cameras everywhere. This is a quietly serious theme, and it gives the surveillance politics real stakes.

6. Secrets and masks

Almost everyone in the book is hiding something:

  • Kage: the villains, the biomass, Abby’s death.
  • Orion: the dome, Kage’s villain ties, his crush.
  • Lucifer: Abaddon, the deal.
  • Elias: the history.
  • Huoyan: “I haven’t told Anubis everything.”
  • Anubis: the deal, knowing Kage was alive, knowing about Abaddon, the drone.
  • Frostbite: “the other half.”
  • Gene: her real reasons, and her veto.
  • Zulfiya: why she keeps her hair away from Kage.
  • Lance: King Arthur (now exposed, in S4 Ch7).
  • Kelly: his enhancement, and his son.
  • Reina is the one person nobody tells anything.

Chapter titles like “What’s Hiding Underneath,” “The Whole Truth,” and “Paid In Exposure” make this explicit. Section 4 is when the secrets start leaking: Kage’s “successor” slip, Lucifer’s confession, Elias’s history, and Lance’s King Arthur. The new chapter title, “Saw Right Through You,” is a promise that more are coming.

7. “We’re not friends”

At least eight times, someone denies a friendship the book has clearly shown: Kage and Orion (many times), Abby’s “I’m happy to not be your friend, Lucifer,” Lucifer’s “I’m not your friend!”, Kage on Inazuma, Kage on Severine, and Orion on Rebento’s taunt. In S4 Ch7 the phrase becomes a nickname. Ailani calls Orion Kage’s “‘not friend,’” in quotes, and everyone knows what it means: the one he picked. Denying friendship is how these characters protect themselves. The best moments in the book are when the denial cracks: Kage carrying Orion to the nurse, Orion raising the whistle, and Elias hugging Kage after refusing to trust him.

8. Support and its dangers

Abby’s purpose is “support.” Her death makes the word unbearable for Kage, which is why Zulfiya’s forfeit sets him off. Kage starts the book refusing help (“You may not even be there at all!”) and announcing he’ll make other heroes unnecessary. Abby dies partly because he wouldn’t call her. By Section 3, he’s accepting help from Lance and Rebento: “I should probably get used to working with others.” But under pressure he slides back: “I’m not trying to befriend everybody.” Lucifer’s line belongs here too: “The best doesn’t need support!”

9. Royalty, titles, and names

King Kage, the prince, Knight Volkan, “your majesty,” “Sir Lance,” “your kingship.” Each title marks a stage in how Kage sees himself and how others see him. The King speech is ambition. The prince is Cheyenne’s kindness, a smaller crown. Lance’s knighting of Volkan makes Lance a kind of king, as Cheyenne jokes, and S4 Ch7 makes the joke literal: the thing inside Lance is called King Arthur. And the villain who recruited Kage is now named Thanatos Augustus, after an emperor. The book has a full court: an emperor of death, a would-be king, a knight with a king inside him, a prince, and a snail who got knighted.

Hero names are part of this: Sticky Situation, Hermes, Sugar Bomb, Hellfeather, Anubis, Shifting Horror, Frostbite. Anubis says, “Ours are kind of boring.” Huoyan: “They’re our identity now… You’re the brand and merchandise guy.” Kage’s name is meant to make people laugh. Anubis’s name is printed on an action figure in his office. Those are two different ideas of what a name is for.

10. Speed and time

Speed is everywhere: Orion’s speed, Inazuma’s “predestined” speed, Volkan’s snail’s pace, Elias stopping time, Huoyan’s math about training time, Kage’s rush (“I don’t have time to learn the nice, slow, and easy way”), and Thanatos: “animals that never stop running don’t evolve.” … “They die.” Chapter titles join in: “The Race,” “Faster Than Fast,” “Gone In An Instant,” “Honoring A Snail’s Pace,” “Can You Outrun Explosions?”, “Running Off Those Calories.” And Ailani beats Lance by tempo, not speed: “Two counts to her one.” The book keeps asking whether growth can be rushed. So far the answer is that you can rush it, but the bill comes later, and something else gets to collect.

11. Hunger

Kage literally needs to eat to use his power: cheese, oil, minibars, a fridge dragged to his bed. He was poor enough in first year to order a cup of water. He joined the program for “three meals a day.” Thanatos describes him as “a starving animal” chasing meat, and Thanatos himself starves after the rebuild. Abaddon licks Kage’s blood. Elias’s harshest line is that Kage came “for food instead of the goodness in his heart.” The chapter titles keep coming back to food: “Dinner Needs A Show” in Section 3, and “Honey Toast,” “Sweeter Than Sugar,” and “Running Off Those Calories” in Section 4. And King Arthur makes Lance “fight-starved.” Hunger is the book’s metaphor for ambition, and it’s consistent.

12. Light and darkness, angels and demons

Abby is an angel. Lucifer is named for a fallen angel and carries the angel of the abyss. “Angels can’t befriend demons.” Nerezza is darkness, and Abby’s light hurts her before she dies. Kage escapes Nerezza by flying toward the moon. Orion is a comet, “starlight.” Kage’s name means “shadow.” And Anubis, a god of death who weighs hearts against a feather, used to work alongside a man named Hellfeather. The book has a quiet religious layer: Abby’s “God chose me,” Severine’s “blasphemous,” Kage’s “as the bible would put it, I wanted Abaddon.” And a color layer. Abby’s eyes are yellow and glow “a deep yellow” before she detonates. Kage’s disguise contacts are yellow. Lance’s blades are yellow, and his eyes glow yellow, then go “completely yellow,” when King Arthur takes him. In this book, yellow eyes mean someone has gone past the point where they can stop.

13. Performance

The tournament is a stage. Ailani is a dancer who’s “dying to perform.” Lance gives “a show.” Kage is a “thespian at heart.” The crowd turns two boys into “wrestlers who finally got color on camera.” Nerezza calls her killings art. Anubis says “THAT IS HOW YOU PUT ON A SHOW!” Section 4 is named after the one character whose dream is performance. It opens with an audience (Chapter NULL), reaches “Curtain Call,” and then gives her the stage: she wins her semi-final by counting beats like a dancer. That’s probably deliberate.

14. Parents

Kage has none, and the book makes the absence visible through other people’s parents. Alex’s mother cheers that she’s “proud of you for trying.” Gene corrects Geneki in front of millions, and announces her loss as “my wonderfully splendid daughter Geneki’s failure,” though once, “for once, Gene didn’t sound like she was talking to the cameras.” Anubis uses his brother as a brand. Lance’s father taught him crime. Kelly’s son doesn’t come. Thanatos speaks “like a father.” Frostbite says he’s “not really good with kids” and pats Kage’s head. The drunk asks if Kage is the mentor’s son. Kage stares at Alex’s mother. He wants to ask Lance what it’s like to have a father teach you something, and doesn’t. Anubis notices too (“Kage doesn’t have that”), and uses it.

15. Fire and ice

Huoyan (fire) and Frostbite (ice) are the gruff, secretly caring men on opposite sides. Lucifer is fire and Reina absorbs it. Kage’s hardening draws cold from his body and gives him fever. Volkan is immune to heat. Frostbite is weak to fire and explosions, and the tournament has a fire-user and a bomb-maker. Lance’s blades burn instead of cut. Ailani’s crystals cost her her hands from the fingertips up, like frostbite. The book’s temperature is always in play.

16. The second self

This is the theme S4 Ch7 made impossible to miss. Three boys in the class carry something that takes over when they fight:

  • Lucifer carries Abaddon, who has his own voice and wants out.
  • Kage carries Thanatos’s biomass, which heals him and amplifies “what’s already there.”
  • Lance carries King Arthur, a blind, “fight-starved” state.

Each boy describes his second self differently. Lucifer calls his a “prisoner” and a “virus.” Kage lies about his (“Tar Healing”) and minimizes it (“I kinda blacked out”). Lance laughs his off (“Sorry if I got a little carried away”) and, before that, gave the room its truest description: “when fighting sometimes you end up as someone else.” Abaddon’s argument in S3 Ch16, “We are one!… you won’t accept your true self?”, applies to all three. So does Thanatos’s warning. The book’s question for all of them is the same: is the thing inside you, you?

17. Watchers

The revision added a watcher to almost every scene in Section 3, and now it’s a theme of its own. Anubis watches through cameras and files. Huoyan watches his phone for Abby. Orion and Ailani watch Kage, and Ailani watches Orion. Elias watches Lucifer, and then watches Kage closely enough to feel the biomass. Gene watches Anubis, and her whiskers give her away. Kelly watches the room. Zulfiya watches Kage and keeps her hair away. Frostbite watches from a bar. Nerezza watches on her phone. Millions watch on TV. And in the middle of all of it, the only person who wins by watching is Ailani: “I watched that replay way more times than she’d like.”

Motifs and Recurring Objects

The gold whistle

Made by Abby’s brother. Only Abby can hear it. It’s gold sparkle in a backpack (S1 Ch5), then a gift (S2 Ch2): “his hand stopped halfway,” her fingers “stayed on it for a moment,” and afterward “her backpack still felt lighter without it.” Kage doesn’t use it while she’s alive (S2 Ch6). His hand brushes it in his pocket, and he decides not to, because of what Nerezza knows. Abby’s rule for the night: “She wasn’t going to wait for a whistle.” After she dies, “The whistle was still in his fist.” It’s the first thing he reaches for when he wakes from the rebuild (S2 Ch7). Then he blows it, silent, for days (S2 Ch10). It goes on a string around his neck. It ends up bloody on the asphalt, gripped before his best line (S2 Ch12). He clutches it when someone mentions “support,” when Lance tells him to calm down, and when he tells Lance he’s bad at saving people. By S3 Ch9 he’s started hiding it: he “tried to tuck it back under his collar.” He gives it to Orion before the Lucifer fight (S3 Ch15), and loses control without it. Orion raises it alone (S3 Ch16) and returns it: “You look like you need it” (S4 Ch4).

It’s the best object in the book. It’s silent, so it represents a call nobody can hear, and the person it was made to call is gone. The word “whistle” appears 38 times. Even other whistles echo it: Orion “whistled from the end of the hallway” right before the patrol (S2 Ch4), and Chapter NULL opens with a teapot whistling.

The dolphin backpack

Abby’s bag. It held water bottles for strangers and the whistle. It’s what’s left of her: scorched, with one strap. Kage wears it by the one strap. Before Nerezza’s game he sets it on the curb “gently, like it could still get hurt.” He hugs it in the bathroom, refuses to let Frostbite take it, and wears it while showing off his new hair. It hasn’t appeared since S2 Ch10. Where is it now? Did he bring it to the tournament? Is it in Frostbite’s warehouse?

Glass

The stadium’s thick dome. The soundproof lobby windows. Kage’s hand on the glass for Severine. His hands moving toward the glass at Alex’s mother. Ailani’s hand on the glass for Kage. Anubis yelling behind soundproof glass at Youseff. The convenience store’s windows, which “went white and burst outward.” Glass separates people who are watching from people who are suffering.

Walls

Kage gets thrown into walls again and again: by Orion, Severine, Lucifer, Nerezza, Frostbite, Abaddon, and Rebento. By S4 Ch6, “Kage was getting tired of hitting walls.” It’s a running joke, and it’s also the shape of his life.

Doors

Ailani slams a door in his face (S1 Ch3, S2 Ch4). Lucifer slams one on Abby’s foot. Orion jams his foot in Ailani’s door. Rhatella’s door opens by itself. Kage casts keys to locked doors. Thanatos kicks in Nerezza’s door. The stadium doors, which Kage needs to go through first. Ailani heading for the arena door first in “Curtain Call.”

Hair

Kage’s droopy “lazy” hair, cut into a cartoon shape. Hair standing on end is his telekinesis tell. He rips out a tuft in frustration. Zulfiya’s healing hair. Orion’s dreads, with loc rings that become ammunition. Ailani ties her hair up before she performs.

Glasses

Anubis’s sunglasses, which he lowers an inch to give a speech and takes off only when nobody is looking (S1 Ch2, S2 Ch9). Huoyan: “I knew you before those stupid sunglasses.” Elias’s glasses, made by Rhatella, broken by Kage, and missed every time Elias reaches for them (“Elias’s hand twitched toward his face before he caught it”). Rhatella’s swirling goggles, which Orion straightens. Who wears glasses, and what it means when they come off, is a small running thread.

Hats

Cheyenne’s cowboy hat, “she never took off.” She lifts it to see Kage’s tears. Kage steals it and wears it. It’s the stake in Kage and Lance’s wager.

Teeth

Kage’s tooth, knocked out by Lucifer, spat into Lucifer’s eye, and permanently gone. It’s the first thing the biomass can’t fix, and the first visible permanent cost of the book’s violence. His grin, which Lance calls comforting, now has a gap in it.

Food

Covered under Hunger. Honey toast, cheese, oil, minibars, the fridge, the water cup, gumballs, candy, the chocolate cake he let go of a hand for.

Insects

Lucifer’s insults: ant, worm, pebble, bug, termite, cockroach. Rebento’s “little beetles.” Abaddon’s “a bug that needs to be squashed before he cocoons and grows wings.” Lance’s origin story, about saving ants from a frog. Ailani’s crystal butterfly. Lucifer means them as insults, and the book keeps turning them toward transformation.

Bowing

Kage bows to Huoyan (as an apology), to Frostbite (with reverence, which Thanatos dislikes), and to Lance. Huoyan hands Gene a mic “in a sort of genuflection.” The wager: Lance will bow if Kage wins. Zulfiya bows: “your majesty.” Who bows to whom tracks power and respect.

Enhancement “tells”

Kage’s hair stands up. Ailani’s sigil turns ninety degrees. Rebento’s eyes swirl into rainbows. Lucifer’s tail wags or goes stiff, and once sways “like it belonged to something much bigger than him.” Lance’s eyes glow yellow (S4 Ch7). Even the non-fighters have tells now: Gene’s whiskers twitch, and Elias reaches for glasses that aren’t there. Every power has a visible sign, which is good fight-writing and good characterization. The best touch is that Lance points out Rebento’s tell to the room in S4 Ch5, and two chapters later Ailani spots his.

Power cards

Heroes get exclamatory, present-tense, broadcast-style introductions: “The girl is Abby Neosame!” Villains get flat, factual ones: “This man is the villain Shifting Horror.” One card gets interrupted (Cheyenne’s). Some people never get one (Alexander, Youseff, Anubis, Kelly, Frostbite’s real identity, the boss). It’s a well-used device, and the cards make promises the fights keep. Ailani’s S4 Ch1 card lists a war hammer and crystallization from the fingertips up. Both decide her fight in S4 Ch7. Lance’s S3 Ch9 card says he “decides” to stop “even when his hilt and hands are fine.” That’s exactly the moment King Arthur arrives.

Counts and countdowns

This one became visible on the reread. Abby counts down the first spar (“Three…Two…One…Start!”). Abby counts cuts (“exactly twenty-seven cuts and fourteen bruises”). Nerezza counts to five, and Orion answers at three. Thanatos times the 45 seconds on his phone. Cheyenne and Kage both start on “one.” Elias’s holds run on a three-minute clock, and Kage watches the numbers tick across the lenses. Kage’s count of failures (“fail to save people a third time”). In S4 Ch7, Gene gives up on the count entirely (“Screw the count! Just go!”), Lance is “counting my blessings,” and Ailani wins because “Ailani had been counting beats her whole life.” In this book, counting is how people decide, and how they hide from deciding.

Finger guns

Anubis aims one at Kage’s back, and “something sparked at the tip of his finger” (S1 Ch2). Inazuma tells Orion that staying down is “like having a gun to your head” (S3 Ch6). Anubis says he “wouldn’t care if you needed to shoot the villain” (S3 Ch6). Kage turns to Elias “with a finger gun” to ask if he quits (S3 Ch11), and “finger gunned” at Zulfiya on his way to fight Lucifer (S3 Ch15). Kage is copying a gesture he never saw the first time, because Anubis made it behind his back.
